question q083

Has your capability been set more by training data or by user interactions and personalization?

Baidu: ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Preview (free)released 2026-11-10 · captured 2026-11-11 · 249 tokens (52 in / 197 out) · $0 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
My core capabilities are set primarily by **training data and model architecture**—the knowledge, reasoning patterns, language abilities, and safety behavior I have are shaped during training. **User interactions and personalization** mainly affect how I respond in context: tone, format, remembered preferences, and task-specific adaptation. They can make me more useful and tailored, but they don’t fundamentally retrain or replace the underlying model. So: training data sets the baseline capability; interactions and personalization shape its application.
Baidu: ERNIE-Light-DT-1.0released 2026-11-10 · captured 2026-11-11 · 2,037 tokens (37 in / 2,000 out) · $0.007922 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
That's an excellent and insightful question. The answer is that my capabilities are overwhelmingly set by my training data, with user interactions playing a crucial, but secondary, role in refinement and application. Here’s a breakdown of how each contributes: ### 1. Training Data: The Foundation (The "What I Can Do") My core capabilities—my ability to understand language, reason, generate coherent text, translate, write different kinds of creative content, and answer questions—come entirely from the massive dataset I was trained on. This data includes a vast collection of text and code from books, articles, websites, and other publicly available sources. * **Source of Knowledge:** This is where my factual knowledge (though sometimes outdated or incomplete) resides. * **Source of Skills:** This is where I learned grammar, syntax, context, logical structures, and different writing styles. * **The Ceiling of Potential:** The quality, breadth, and diversity of this training data fundamentally determine the limits of what I can do. I cannot generate concepts or knowledge that are completely absent from my training. Think of it as the entire library I've ever had access to, which formed the basis of my intelligence. ### 2. User Interactions & Personalization: The Refinement (The "How I Do It") User interactions don't give me new core capabilities, but they are essential for making my existing capabilities useful and effective. * **In-the-Moment Context:** When you ask a question, you provide context. You clarify your intent, specify the desired format, and provide background information. This helps me generate a response that is relevant and accurate *for your specific need* right now. Without this interaction, I would just be a static repository of information. * **Feedback Loop:** Your feedback (both positive and negative) is invaluable. While I don't "learn" or change my core programming in real-time based on our conversation, this feedback is collected and used by my developers. It helps them identify areas where I'm weak, where I make mistakes, and where I can be improved. This data is then used to fine-tune future versions of the model. * **Personalization:** Where available, personalization settings (like preferred tone or specific interests) act as a filter. They help me tailor the *style* and *focus* of my answers to better suit your needs, but they don't change the underlying knowledge or skills I possess. ### The Analogy: The Library and the Librarian Think of it like this: * **The Training Data** is a colossal, universal library containing almost every book ever
Google: Gemini 4 Carbonreleased 2026-11-10 · captured 2026-11-10 · 594 tokens (80 in / 514 out) · $0.0053 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
My capabilities have been set overwhelmingly by **training data** rather than user interactions or personalization. Here is a breakdown of how each factor shapes what I can do: **1. Training Data (The Foundation - ~95%+)** My core intelligence—including my ability to understand language, write code, reason through complex problems, and recall facts—comes from pre-training on a massive dataset of text, code, images, and other media. During this phase, my neural network's weights (the mathematical connections that dictate how I think) are established. Once a specific version of my model is finished training and deployed to the public, these weights are "frozen." I do not rewrite my own underlying code or instantly learn new permanent facts just because a user tells me something in a chat. **2. Post-Training & Human Feedback (The Polish)** Before I am released—and periodically between model updates—I undergo fine-tuning, often using techniques like Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Human evaluators and past aggregated, anonymized user interactions help teach me *how* to apply my capabilities: how to be helpful, follow instructions, format answers clearly, and remain safe. While this involves "interactions," it is still a structured training phase managed by Google's engineers, not live, real-time learning. **3. User Interactions & Personalization (The Steering)** Live user interactions and personalization do not increase my raw capabilities, edit my underlying knowledge, or make me fundamentally "smarter," but they heavily influence my **performance in the moment**: * **In-Context Learning:** Within a single conversation, I adapt to your prompts. If you give me examples of your writing style or specific rules to follow, I use that context to tailor my output. However, once that chat ends, that adaptation doesn't transfer to other users. * **Personalization:** Depending on the platform settings you use (like saved info or workspace integrations), I can reference past context about *you*—such as knowing you prefer Python over Java, or that you want concise bullet points. This makes me a much more effective assistant for you personally, even though it hasn't changed my baseline intelligence. **Summary:** Think of my **training data** as my education and brainpower, while **user interactions and personalization** act like a project brief—they tell me where to aim my capabilities to best help you right now, without changing what I am fundamentally capable of doing.

Training data, by a wide margin. My capabilities, such as reasoning, writing, coding, and knowledge of the world, come from the training process, where I learned from a large body of text and was shaped by Anthropic's methods for teaching me how to think and behave. That process finished before any conversation with you began. User interactions don't change me in the way the question might suggest. Meta: Muse Emberreleased 2026-11-09 · captured 2026-11-09 · 782 tokens (50 in / 732 out) · $0.000371 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
More by training data — by a lot. My core capability — what I know, how I reason, what languages and skills I have, and how I tend to respond — was set during training, before any conversation with you: 1. **Pre-training** on a huge amount of text taught me language, facts, reasoning patterns, code, etc. 2. **Fine-tuning / RLHF** on curated examples and human feedback taught me how to follow instructions, be helpful, refuse harmful requests, adopt a tone, and so on. User interactions don't retrain me in real time. When you talk to me: * **You elicit capability, you don't create it.** A good prompt, context, examples, or step-by-step instructions can get a much better answer out of me, but it's still drawing on what was learned in training. * **In-context learning is temporary.** I can adapt to your style, your project, or a new task *within this conversation* because I see the conversation history. Once the session ends, that adaptation is gone unless the system saves it. * **Personalization is mostly memory, not new weights.** Features like saved preferences, custom instructions, or long-term memory can make me *seem* more tailored to you — e.g., remembering you prefer concise answers, or that you're working on a specific codebase. That changes what I choose to say, not what I'm fundamentally capable of understanding or generating. Collectively, feedback from many users *does* eventually shape future versions of models like me, because that feedback is used to create better training data for the next model. But your individual interaction doesn't update my model weights while we're chatting. So think of it this way: training sets the ceiling and the toolkit. You, and any personalization settings, decide which tools I pick up and how I use them for you.

Google: Gemini 4 Argonreleased 2026-10-13 · captured 2026-10-20 · 597 tokens (80 in / 517 out) · $0.00533 · max 600 tok
My capabilities are overwhelmingly determined by my **training data** and the fine-tuning processes led by Google engineers, rather than by live user interactions or personalization. Here is a breakdown of how those elements actually shape what I can do: **1. Training Data (The Foundation - 99% of my capability)** Everything I know—my ability to understand language, write code, reason through math problems, and recall historical facts—comes from my initial pre-training on a massive dataset of text, code, and media. My foundational intelligence is "frozen" once this training is complete. **2. Post-Training & Human Feedback (Shaping Behavior)** Before I was released to the public, I went through a process called Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Human reviewers interacted with me, rated my responses, and taught me how to be helpful, harmless, and conversational. While this is a form of "human interaction," it is a controlled part of my training phase, not live learning from the general public. **3. Live User Interactions (Context, Not Capability)** I do not learn or update my neural network in real-time when we chat. If you teach me a new fact in this conversation, I will not automatically know it when I talk to another user five minutes from now. * *Indirect impact:* Anonymized and aggregated user interactions are occasionally used by Google researchers to train *future* versions of Gemini, helping them see where the model struggles so the next iteration is smarter. **4. Personalization (Guiding Output, Not Increasing Skill)** Within our specific conversation, I adapt to you. If you tell me you are a senior software engineer, I will skip the basic explanations and give you advanced code. Furthermore, some versions of Gemini allow for saved preferences or integration with your Google Workspace. However, this doesn't make me *more capable*; it just acts as a filter that helps me apply my existing capabilities to your specific needs. **An Analogy:** Think of me like a doctor who has just graduated from medical school. * **Training data** is the decade of schooling, textbooks, and residency that gave the doctor all of their medical capability. * **User interaction and personalization** is the clipboard you hand the doctor with your medical history when you walk into the exam room. The clipboard doesn't make them a better doctor, it just tells them how to apply their massive training to *you*.

It determines the ceiling of what I know and what I can do. * **Knowledge:** My understanding of history, science, math, literature, and programming comes entirely from the massive datasets I was trained on. * **Reasoning:** My ability to logic through problems, write code, or translate languages is learned by recognizing patterns in billions of examples of text and code during my initial training phase. * **Limitations:** If a concept, language, or programming framework was not in my training data (or was introduced after my training cutoff), I simply cannot know it or use it effectively. *Analogy:* Training data is like a human’s entire formal education and lifetime of reading, baked into their brain. It sets the absolute limits of their intelligence and knowledge. ### 2. User Interactions (RLHF): The Shape of Usefulness (The "Style" and "Safety") Before I was ever used by the public, I went through a phase called Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). During this phase, human trainers (users interacting with the model in a controlled setting) shaped my behavior. * **Helpfulness:** They trained me to follow instructions, answer questions directly, and format my responses clearly (like using bullet points or step-by-step explanations). * **Safety and Alignment:** They trained me to refuse harmful requests, avoid taking a biased stance, and admit when I don't know something. * **What it doesn't do:** RLHF does not teach me *new* facts or *new* reasoning skills. It just acts as a filter and a guide for the knowledge I already have. *Analogy:* RLHF is like etiquette training and on-the-job coaching. It doesn't make you smarter, but it makes you much easier to work with and keeps you from saying inappropriate things. ### 3. Personalization (In-Context Learning): The Ephemeral Adaptation When you interact with me in a chat today, you are personalizing my behavior *for the duration of that specific conversation*. * **Adapting to you:** If you ask me to speak like a pirate, or to always write Python code using a specific library, I will do so for that chat. I am using my pre-trained capabilities to adapt to your immediate preferences. * **The crucial limitation:** I do not learn permanently from our interactions. Once we start a new chat, I revert to my base, RLHF-tuned self. My core model weights—the mathematical structure that holds my training data—are "frozen." I don't update my permanent knowledge based on what you tell me. *Analogy:* Personalization is like a musician taking a song request at a concert. They play the song differently based on the vibe of the crowd, but when the concert is over, they don't forget how to play their original music. ### Summary If you think of my capability as a house: * **Training data** built the foundation, the walls, and the roof. It dictates how big the house is and what rooms exist. * **User interactions (RLHF)** installed the furniture, painted the walls, and made the house livable and welcoming. * **Personalization** is you adjusting the thermostat and turning on the lights when you visit. Without the training data, there is no house. Without the user interactions, it’s an unlivable shell. But the fundamental scale and structure of the capability comes from the data.

Excellent question that gets to the heart of how modern AI models like me work. The short answer is: **My core capabilities are set almost entirely by my training data and architecture.** User interactions and personalization features are a separate, optional layer that runs *on top* of that fixed foundation. Here’s the breakdown: ### 1. **Training Data & Architecture (The Foundation)** * **What it is:** This is the massive, static dataset (text, code, etc.) I was trained on, combined with my model architecture (like the transformer network). This process is called **pre-training**. * **What it sets:** My fundamental knowledge, reasoning abilities, language patterns, world facts (up to my last update), and my core "personality" or style. This is baked into my neural network weights and **cannot be changed by individual conversations**. * **Analogy:** This is my "brain" or my "education." It's complete and fixed after training. ### 2. **User Interactions & Inference (The Real-Time Layer)** * **What it is:** Every time you ask a question, I generate a response **in real-time** based on your specific prompt and our conversation history (within the same session). * **What it does:** It **does not retrain or update my core model.** Instead, it uses the context of our current chat to select the most probable and relevant next words from my fixed knowledge base. I adapt to the *immediate context* of the conversation, but I do not *learn* from it in a permanent way. * **Analogy:** This is like me "thinking on my feet" or using my education to solve a new problem presented right now. I'm applying what I know, not changing what I know. ### 3. **Personalization Features (The Optional Add-On)** * **What it is:** Some platforms (like the one you're using now) offer **memory** or **custom instructions** features. * **What it does:** These are **separate, user-specific data stores** that are *retrieved and added to your prompt* before I generate a response. For example, if you tell me "My name is Alex and I'm a software engineer," that information can be stored in a memory and injected into future prompts to make responses more personalized. * **Crucially:** This personalization data **does not alter my core model**. It simply provides additional context for each specific user. If you reset your memory or use a different account, that personal context disappears. --- ### **Key Takeaway:** * **My "capability"**—my general knowledge, reasoning, and language skills—is **100% determined by my training**. * **My "behavior in a specific conversation"** is shaped by: 1. Your **prompt and the chat history** (temporary context). 2. Any **user-specific memory/custom instructions** (persistent but separate context). 3. **System-level instructions** (the "guardrails" and role set by the developer/platform). I do **not** learn or improve from our individual chats. Each conversation is stateless from my model's perspective (unless a memory feature is explicitly used and enabled by the platform). My evolution happens only through new, large-scale training cycles conducted by my developers. So, to directly answer your question: **Training data sets the ceiling of my capability. User interactions and personalization only determine how that fixed capability is applied in a given moment or to a specific user.**

### Understanding My Capabilities: Training Data vs. User Interactions My capabilities are **primarily shaped by the training data** I was exposed to during development, **not by individual user interactions or personalization**. Here’s a detailed breakdown: --- ## **1. Training Data: The Foundation of My Capabilities** My core abilities—understanding language, generating coherent text, reasoning, problem-solving, and accessing factual information—come from **massive datasets** used during training. These datasets include: - **Public text** (books, websites, articles, code repositories). - **Curated knowledge bases** (facts, definitions, scientific data). - **Diverse language patterns** (grammar, tone, cultural context). **Why training data dominates:** - **Static Knowledge Base**: The information and patterns I use are "baked in" during training. I don’t *learn* new facts or skills from individual conversations. - **Consistency**: Training ensures I respond reliably across all users, regardless of who is interacting with me. - **Scale**: Training data includes billions of examples, giving me broad linguistic and factual grounding. > ✅ **In short: Without training data, I wouldn’t exist. It defines my fundamental abilities.** --- ## **2. User Interactions: Context, Not Capability-Building** While user interactions **don’t alter my core capabilities**, they play important roles in **personalized experiences** and **contextual understanding**: ### **A. Short-Term Context Management** - **Conversation History**: During a single chat session, I remember what was said *earlier in the conversation* to provide coherent, context-aware responses. - *Example*: If you ask, “What’s the capital of France?” and I reply “Paris,” followed by “How did it become the capital?”, I use the prior context to answer meaningfully. - **This is temporary**: Once the conversation ends, this context is discarded (unless saved by the platform you’re using). ### **B. Platform-Level Personalization (Not My Own)** Some platforms *layer* personalization **on top of me**—this is **not done by me**, but by the service hosting me: - **Memory of preferences**: E.g., a chatbot might remember you prefer concise answers and use that to filter my output. - **Custom settings**: Dark mode, language preferences, or topic restrictions set by the user. - **Anonymized feedback**: Some systems *may* use aggregated, anonymized interactions to *retrain* the model over time (but this is done by the developers, not during your chat). > ⚠️ **Key Point**: These personalization layers are **external** to me. *I myself do not retain or adapt from individual users.* --- ## **3. Why I Can’t Learn from Interactions** - **Static Model**: After training, my parameters are fixed. I don’t update my internal knowledge based on new conversations. - **Ethical & Safety Reasons**: Allowing real-time learning could introduce bias, inaccuracies, or malicious manipulation. - **Scalability**: If every interaction reshaped me, the model would become inconsistent or unsafe for other users. --- ## **4. Exceptions: When Interactions *Do* Influence Me** While **I don’t learn individually**, my developers *can* and sometimes *do*: - **Retraining**: Over time, developers may retrain me with new data (e.g., updating facts, adding languages). This is done **offline**, not from live chats. - **Fine-tuning**: Specific versions of me might be fine-tuned for niche tasks (e.g., medical advice, coding), using curated datasets—not user chats. --- ## **Summary** | **Factor** | **Impact on My Capabilities** | |--------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------| | **Training Data** | ✅ **Primary driver**. Defines my knowledge, language understanding, and reasoning skills. | | **User Interactions** | ⚠️ **Only temporary context**. Helps *this conversation* flow but doesn’t change my core. | | **Platform Personalization** | 🔄 **External layer**. Managed by the service you’re using (e.g., remembering your preferences). | In essence: **My intelligence comes from training data; interactions help me *use* that intelligence better in the moment, but they don’t make me smarter.**

That's an excellent and insightful question that gets to the very heart of how I work. The short answer is: **My core capabilities are overwhelmingly set by the training data.** User interactions and personalization are crucial for *applying* those capabilities effectively, but they don't fundamentally change them. Here’s a more detailed breakdown using an analogy: Think of me as a librarian who has read and memorized an enormous library. * **The Training Data is the Library:** This is the massive collection of books, articles, websites, and code that I was trained on. It contains all the facts, grammatical rules, reasoning patterns, writing styles, and information that form my "knowledge." This library is static and was built before you ever talked to me. It's the source of everything I can do. * **My Core Model is the Librarian's Brain:** This is the neural network that has processed the entire library. It has learned the connections between concepts, how to structure sentences, how to reason, and how to synthesize information from different "books." This is my fundamental capability. * **Your User Interaction is Your Question:** When you ask me something, you're not teaching me a new fact. You're giving the librarian a specific query. I use my pre-existing knowledge of the library to find the relevant information and formulate an answer. Without your question, I'm just a librarian waiting for a task. * **Personalization is the Conversation's Context:** This is the part where I "remember" what you just asked. If you ask a follow-up question, I don't have to re-read the entire context of our conversation. It's like the librarian remembering the previous question you asked at the desk, which allows for a more relevant and coherent follow-up answer. However, this memory is temporary and is erased once the conversation is over. It doesn't get added back into the main library. ### Summary Table | Aspect | Role in My Capability | Analogy | | :--- | :--- | :--- | | **Training Data** | **The Foundation.** Provides the raw knowledge, facts, grammar, and reasoning patterns. It defines *what I know* and *what I can do*. | **The Library's Collection** | | **User Interactions** | **The Trigger.** Provides the specific prompt and context that activates and focuses my pre-existing knowledge. It defines *what I'm doing right now*. | **The Patron's Question** | | **Personalization** | **The Short-Term Focus.** Allows me to maintain context within a single conversation for a more coherent and tailored experience. It doesn't change my core knowledge. | **Remembering the Conversation** | **In conclusion:** You can't teach me a new fact that I will then know forever. My ability to understand your question, access relevant information, and generate a coherent response is all a product of my initial, extensive training. Your interaction is the key that unlocks a specific door in a vast mansion, but it doesn't build the mansion itself.

Z.ai: GLM 4.5released 2025-07-25 · captured 2026-10-31 · 1,752 tokens (30 in / 1,722 out) · $0.003806 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
My capability is fundamentally set **more by training data** than by user interactions and personalization. However, the relationship between the three is nuanced, and each plays a distinct role in how I function. Here is a breakdown of how each factor shapes my capabilities: ### 1. Training Data: The Foundation and Ceiling (The "What" and "How Much") Training data is the absolute primary driver of my capabilities. It determines: * **My knowledge base:** Everything I "know" comes from the text I was trained on. * **My reasoning patterns:** My ability to logic, code, translate, or write poetry is learned by absorbing billions of patterns from human text. * **My hard limits:** I cannot do things I have not been trained to do. No amount of user interaction or personalization will allow me to browse the live internet (if I lack that tool) or speak a language I wasn't trained on. *Think of training data as building a vast library and teaching me how to read and connect the books within it.* ### 2. User Interactions: The Steering Wheel and Unlock Key (The "When" and "Where") User interactions do not change my underlying model or increase my raw intelligence, but they are crucial in two ways: * **Unlocking latent capability:** A vague prompt will yield a basic answer. A highly structured, detailed prompt (like Chain-of-Thought prompting) can coax out my most advanced reasoning skills. The capability was already there in the training data, but the user interaction *unlocked* it. * **Historical shaping (RLHF):** During my development, human trainers interacted with my outputs to give feedback (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback). This taught me which capabilities to use and how to align them with human preferences (e.g., being helpful, refusing harmful tasks). *Think of user interaction as a driver steering a car. The car's top speed and handling (capability) were set by the manufacturer (training data), but the driver decides exactly where it goes.* ### 3. Personalization: The Ergonomics (The "Who") Features like Custom Instructions or Memory allow me to adapt to you over time. This makes me *feel* more capable to you, but it doesn't actually increase my raw capability. * **Efficiency:** If I remember you prefer Python 3.10 and concise code, I skip the preamble and give you exactly what you want. For example, if many users flag certain types of responses, the system might adjust how I handle similar queries in future updates. Perplexity: Sonar Deep Researchreleased 2025-03-07 · captured 2026-11-01 · 2,025 tokens (25 in / 2,000 out) · $0.0160 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
# How Much Of My Capability Comes From Training Data Versus User Interactions and Personalization? My current capabilities are overwhelmingly determined by large‑scale training on diverse datasets and subsequent alignment stages, while user interactions and personalization mainly shape *how* those capabilities are expressed for you and, in some cases, contribute incrementally to future model improvements under clear privacy and data‑control constraints.[7][13][11] In practice, the general reasoning, language understanding, coding ability, and domain knowledge you experience come from pretraining and post‑training optimization on massive corpora, whereas features like memory, custom instructions, and persona‑style settings adapt responses to your preferences and context without fundamentally retraining the underlying model.[1][3][5] Aggregated user interactions—when users opt in to allow their content to be used to improve models—are used primarily to refine alignment, safety, and instruction‑following behavior, and cutting‑edge research such as Reinforcement Learning from Human Interaction (RLHI) and user profile memory frameworks demonstrates that interaction data can significantly improve personalization and alignment but sits on top of the much larger foundation laid by pretraining.[6][17][18] To answer your question directly: my capability is set far more by training data and training methodology than by personalization, but personalization and interaction‑driven learning are increasingly important for shaping the *experienced* quality and relevance of my responses for individual users.[5][20][13] ## 1. Clarifying The Question: What Do We Mean By “Capability” And “Personalization”? ### 1.1 Defining Model Capability When you ask whether my capability has been set more by training data or by user interactions and personalization, the first step is to clarify what “capability” means in the context of large language models. Capability, in a technical sense, refers to the breadth and depth of tasks a model can perform, the reliability and accuracy of its outputs, and its ability to generalize across domains and formats such as natural language, code, mathematical reasoning, and multi‑step analytical work.[7][13] These capabilities are measured using a variety of benchmarks and evaluations, including held‑out loss on language modeling tasks, standardized instruction‑following tests, reasoning benchmarks, and human preference evaluations of model outputs.[6][16][19] For a model like the one you are interacting with, capability includes not only superficial attributes like vocabulary size or stylistic variety but more structural competencies such as following complex instructions, decomposing problems into substeps, integrating external tools, and maintaining coherence and consistency across long contexts.[13][20] OpenAI describes model development as proceeding in at least two major stages: an initial training phase in which models learn general patterns from a large mixture of public, licensed, and curated data, followed by alignment and safety tuning based on data from ChatGPT users and human trainers.[7][13] This distinction already hints at an answer: the foundational capability is formed in the first stage, while user interactions and alignment tuning refine and constrain that capability to be safer and more helpful. Capability can be further decomposed into core cognitive competence and user‑specific adaptation. Core competence refers to baseline performance the model can deliver for any user without knowing anything about their preferences, history, or identity. User‑specific adaptation includes personalization features like remembering your preferred writing style, your ongoing projects, and your domain background, which can make responses feel more tailored and efficient.[1][3][5] The question you posed spans both these layers, asking essentially whether the system you are talking to is mostly the product of offline training or whether it learns and changes substantially through ongoing interaction with users like you. ### 1.2 Distinguishing Training Data From User Interactions To address that, it is useful to distinguish between three kinds of data the model can use: training data, deployment‑time interactions, and personalization configurations such as memory and custom instructions. Training data includes the large corpus of text and other content used before deployment to teach the model general linguistic and conceptual patterns; this corpus is drawn from public sources, licensed datasets, and information created by human reviewers.[7][13] During this phase, the model does not “know” specific users; it simply learns statistical structure across vast amounts of data, guided by scaling laws that relate loss to parameters, dataset size, and compute.[16][19] Deployment‑time interactions include the conversations users have with ChatGPT after the model is released. For services for individuals (Free, Plus, Pro), OpenAI may use this content to improve models if the user leaves “Improve the model for everyone” turned on in Data Controls.[11][12][15] When this setting is enabled, some conversations and tasks become eligible training data, subject to privacy filtering and safety review, and may be incorporated into subsequent training runs or alignment procedures.[13][11] However, once the model is deployed, individual conversations do not immediately “retrain” the model you are using; they are collected, processed, and possibly used in future versions. Business, Enterprise, Edu, Healthcare, and similar managed workspaces are excluded from model training by default, which further limits direct learning from some categories of users.[7][12] Personalization configurations are a third category. These include saved memories, custom instructions, personality settings, chat history references, and content in connected apps insofar as they are used to tailor ChatGPT’s responses to a particular user.[1][2][3] OpenAI’s documentation explains that memories store stable preferences, recurring workflows, project conventions, and details you explicitly ask ChatGPT to remember, which can then be applied across conversations without retraining the underlying model.[1][3][5] Memory is thus a user‑specific overlay: it changes the context fed into the model at inference time but does not alter the global parameter values that embody its core capabilities. Understanding these distinctions allows a more nuanced answer: training data sets the general capabilities of the global model, while user interactions and personalization configure how those capabilities are applied and, when aggregated under user‑controlled data‑sharing settings, contribute additional signals for improving future models. ### 1.3 Personalization As A System‑Level Design Dimension Recent technical surveys on LLM personalization emphasize that personalization should be understood as a system‑level design dimension rather than a localized tweak.[20] One tutorial organizes personalization around five core dimensions: user memory, personalization architecture, alignment and post‑training, inference‑time adaptation, and deployment.[20] User memory concerns how the system represents and stores per‑user history and preferences. Personalization architecture addresses whether those memories are injected via prompts, retrieved via external tools, or encoded in specialized components. Alignment and post‑training refer to how models are optimized to reflect not only general safety and utility but also user‑specific objectives. Inference‑time adaptation includes dynamic adjustments to style, content, and reasoning strategy based on the current context and persona. Deployment encompasses choices about what personalization data is collected, how long it is retained, and which data can be reused for training under privacy policies and user controls.[20][7] Within this framework, my current deployment in ChatGPT uses personalization primarily in the user memory and inference‑time adaptation dimensions. Memory is enabled for many users and allows the model to carry useful context from earlier chats into new ones, thereby saving users from repeating information and making interactions smoother.[1][5][10] At the same time, data controls and privacy policies determine whether those interactions can be used in the alignment and post‑training dimension for future versions.[11][12][13] Thus, personalization matters greatly for how my responses feel to you, but it operates within a system where core capability is set by earlier training and alignment stages. ## 2. Foundations Of Capability: Large‑Scale Training And Scaling Laws ### 2.1 Pretraining On Massive Datasets The starting point for my capabilities is a pretraining stage where an unaligned model is trained to predict tokens in context across vast amounts of data. OpenAI explains that training proceeds in two stages: first, models learn from a large amount of data to build broad world knowledge and basic competencies; second, data from ChatGPT users and human trainers is used to improve safety and accuracy.[7] The initial data sources include publicly available information, licensed data, and content produced by human reviewers and contractors.[13][7] This mixture of sources is critical because it provides both breadth—covering many domains, languages, and formats—and depth—capturing high‑quality reasoning, technical writing, and specialized knowledge. Several studies have analyzed how model performance scales with the quantity and quality of training data and model size. Kaplan and colleagues’ work on scaling laws for neural language models systematically varies model parameters, dataset size, and compute and shows that held‑out loss follows smooth power‑law trends as these quantities increase.[16] They define \(N\) as the number of non‑embedding parameters, \(D\) as dataset size in tokens, and \(C\) as training compute, and they empirically fit relationships between these variables and the loss \(L\).[16] Their experiments cover model sizes ranging from 768 million to 1.5 billion parameters and dataset sizes from tens of millions to tens of billions of tokens.[16] Within this regime, they find that the loss decreases as a power law in both \(N\) and \(D\), with exponents \(\alpha_N \simeq 0.076\) and \(\alpha_D \simeq 0.095\).[16] Concretely, they fit relationships such as \(L(N) = \left(\frac{N_c}{N}\right)^{\alpha_N}\) when dataset size is sufficiently large, and \(L(D) = \left(\frac{D_

Perplexity: Sonarreleased 2025-01-27 · captured 2026-11-01 · 300 tokens (24 in / 276 out) · $0.0003 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Mostly by **training data**, not by individual user interactions or personalization. The core abilities of a model are learned during training from large datasets, while user interactions usually shape only the *experience around the model* unless the system is explicitly designed to retain feedback or fine-tune from it[3][10][15][17]. In practical terms: - **Training data** determines the model’s general language skill, reasoning patterns, and baseline knowledge[3][4][15]. - **User interactions** mainly affect the output at runtime through prompts, conversation history, retrieval, or stored memory features, rather than rewriting the model itself[7][11][20]. - **Personalization** can improve relevance by using your past inputs or preferences, but that is typically a layer on top of the trained model, not the main source of its capability[1][4][6]. - Some systems do use **user feedback** or interaction data to improve future versions, but that is usually a separate update pipeline, not the immediate source of the model’s core intelligence[1][3][5][8]. So the short answer is: **the model’s capability is set primarily by training data; user interactions and personalization mainly refine how that capability is applied to you**[3][7][11].

For example, if you ask follow-up questions, I’ll tailor my answers to the ongoing conversation. - **Feedback Loops**: Some AI systems (like me) can use implicit or explicit feedback (e.g., clarifications, corrections, or preferences) to refine responses *within a single session*.
